[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.2, F32.1, F32.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I just… I don’t see a way forward anymore. I’ve been trying to keep things together, especially with the studio and the payments and all that, and every time somebody talks about another plan or another way to catch up, it just feels like words. Like it looks good on paper maybe, but in real life I can’t make it work.\n\nI’ve always been someone who figures things out by working harder, you know, putting in more hours, taking the job nobody else wants, fixing what’s broken piece by piece. But lately it feels like no matter how hard I work, nothing actually gets better. I can’t picture the studio getting back on track, and honestly I can’t really picture my life getting back on track either. It’s like I’m standing there looking at this mess and I know I’m supposed to say, “Okay, here’s the next step,” but I don’t believe there is one anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed if your mood has been consistently low or if you’ve lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. It’s not just a bad day here and there. Most mornings I wake up and it’s like this heavy, gray feeling is already there before I even open my eyes all the way. Like I haven’t even had a chance to think about the day yet, and my mood has already decided for me.\n\nAnd the stuff I used to care about… the shop, especially. I used to love being around the wood, the smell of it, looking at a rough piece and being able to picture what it could turn into. That used to settle me, honestly. But now I can be standing right there in the shop, surrounded by all of it, and it just feels flat. Like there’s no spark in it. I’ll pick something up and then just… stop. I have to fight not to just stand there staring, because nothing in me really connects to it the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like you’re not good enough or that things are your fault, even when others might not see it that way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s like, when something goes wrong at the studio, I don’t just see it as one thing that went wrong. If I’m fitting a joint and it doesn’t line up clean, or I measure something and it’s off, even a little, I don’t think, “Okay, fix the cut, adjust it, move on.” I go straight to, “What kind of carpenter can’t get this right?” And then it keeps going. Like, “Maybe I’m not as good as I thought I was. Maybe I’ve been fooling myself this whole time.”\n\nSame with the business side. If I miss a bid deadline or I don’t get back to someone fast enough, I don’t just think I messed up that one part. I start thinking I had no business trying to run my own studio in the first place. Like I took something I cared about and proved I wasn’t capable of handling it.\n\nOther people might say, you know, mistakes happen, or everybody gets behind. But in my head it feels bigger than that. It feels like it says something about me. Like I’m not dependable, or I’m not skilled enough, or I should’ve known better than to think I could make it work.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to live anymore or if it would just be easier if you weren’t around?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI mean, I hear what you’re asking, and I know it’s important, but when you say “not wanting to live anymore,” I’m not sure if what I feel counts as that, exactly. It’s more like… I get so tired of being in my own head, and tired of waking up to the same pile of things I don’t know how to fix. Sometimes the thought is less “I want to die” and more “I don’t want to have to keep doing this like this.” If that makes sense.\n\nAnd the “easier if I weren’t around” part… yeah, that thought kind of brushes up against me sometimes, I guess. Not in a way I’ve sat down and made sense of, and not something I know how to talk about without feeling ashamed. It’s just this ugly feeling that I’m taking up space or making things harder, and then I try to push it away because it scares me a little to even say it out loud.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I’m not sure what box that goes in. I just know I’ve been feeling worn down enough that those kinds of thoughts don’t feel as far away as they used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that more directly than I did. I don’t think I can say I’ve made some clear decision or anything like that. It’s more… the thoughts scare me because they get close to that line, and I don’t always know what to call them.\n\nHurting myself… I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there and thought it through in a concrete way, if that’s what you mean. But there are moments when I feel so worn down that I don’t feel like I trust my own thinking the way I used to. Like I’ll be standing there with all that heaviness in my chest, and it’s not exactly a plan, it’s not like I’m calmly deciding something, but there’s this thought of, “I can’t keep feeling like this.” And then I get ashamed for even having that thought.\n\nEnding my life… saying it that way makes my stomach drop. I don’t want to tell you yes if yes means I’m planning something, because I’m not saying that. But I also don’t want to lie and make it sound like everything in my head is safe and tidy, because it isn’t. It’s more like those thoughts come near me sometimes, and I try to back away from them. I don’t really know how dangerous that makes it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that the only way to escape how you’re feeling is to end your life, even if you haven’t made a specific plan?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say it exactly like that. The “only way” part feels… I don’t know, it feels too definite, like I’ve sat there and come to a conclusion, and I don’t think I have. I haven’t made a plan, and I’m not sitting here thinking, “This is what I’m going to do.”\n\nBut I have had moments where it feels like I just want out. Out of the feeling, out of the pressure in my head, out of waking up and already feeling like I’m behind or failing before I’ve even done anything. And in those moments, my mind kind of goes toward that dark edge, where it feels like maybe there isn’t another way to get relief. I don’t like saying that. It makes me feel ashamed, and it scares me.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it believing ending my life is the only way, exactly. But I’ve been close enough to that kind of thought that I can’t honestly say it never happens. It’s more like the thought shows up as an escape hatch, and then I try not to look at it too long.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever started thinking about how you might end your life, even if you didn’t go through with it or make a firm plan?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I don’t think I have anything clear to tell you there.\n\nLike, I haven’t sat down and thought, “This is how I would do it,” or worked anything out in my head. At least not in a way that feels like a plan. Sometimes my mind gets kind of close to that area and then I almost pull back from it, because it feels like if I keep going down that road, it’s going to become more real than I want it to be.\n\nIt’s hard because when you ask “how,” part of me wants to say no, because I don’t have some specific answer. But another part of me worries that even being near those thoughts means something. I don’t know if that makes sense. It’s more like I get overwhelmed and want everything to stop, and then I try not to let my mind fill in the blanks.\n\nSo… no firm plan. I don’t think I’ve really let myself think it through like that. But I’m scared that the thoughts have gotten close enough that I’m even having to answer this question.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself thinking about death or dying, even if you didn’t have a plan or intend to do anything about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to separate that from the other stuff, honestly. Death… dying… those words float through my head sometimes, but I don’t always know if it’s me thinking about death or just thinking about not having to keep doing this. If that makes sense.\n\nI don’t sit there imagining some clear scene or anything like that. It’s more vague. Like I’ll be driving home, or lying there when everything’s quiet, and the thought is just… “What if I didn’t have to wake up tomorrow?” And then I feel this horrible mix of relief and fear, and then guilt for even letting that thought exist. It’s not like I’m saying I want to act on it. I don’t think that’s what I mean. But yeah, I guess death comes into my mind as this kind of blank ending, like a way for the noise to stop.\n\nI hate admitting that because it sounds worse out loud than it feels in my head sometimes. In my head it can be almost passive, like I’m not reaching for it, it just shows up. But then when you ask me directly, I can hear how serious it is. So… yes, I’ve thought about death or dying, but not in a way where I’ve made a plan or decided I’m going to do something. It’s more like the thought is there in the background when I’m really worn down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still, to the point that others might notice?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think anyone has said to me, “You’re moving slow,” or “You can’t sit still,” not in those words anyway.\n\nI mean, I’m not always a great judge of how I come across. Sometimes I feel like I’m just… dragging myself through things, but I don’t know if that means I’m actually moving slower in a way somebody else would notice, or if it’s more just how it feels inside. And with sitting still, I don’t think I’m pacing around all the time or anything like that. I might shift around or feel uncomfortable, but I don’t know that it’s obvious.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I haven’t really noticed it to that point, and I don’t think other people have pointed it out. If they have, I either didn’t take it that way or I wasn’t paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been keeping close enough track to say something definite, like “I lost this many pounds” or anything like that.\n\nMy appetite… I don’t know. Some days I eat kind of normally, or at least I think I do, and other days I realize I didn’t really pay attention to food much. But I can’t say for sure that it’s a big change, or that I’ve been eating much less than usual in some clear way. Nobody’s said I look thinner, and my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything.\n\nI guess if you’re asking whether I’ve noticed weight loss without trying, I don’t think so. Not that I’ve noticed. I probably wouldn’t be the best person to judge unless it was really obvious, but no, nothing specific comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your energy or feel fatigued most days, even when you haven’t done much physically or mentally?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That one I can answer pretty clearly. I feel tired most days, and it’s not the normal kind of tired where you can point to a hard day and say, well, that makes sense.\n\nIt’s like… by midmorning, sometimes my arms already feel like I’ve been hauling lumber all day, even if I haven’t done much yet. I’ll be trying to measure something or make a cut, or even just return a couple calls, and it feels like I’m pushing through mud. Like every little step has weight to it. And I’ll think, “How am I this wiped out already?” because it’s not even lunch.\n\nEven when I’ve been in bed a full night, it doesn’t really reset me. I can get up and technically I slept, but my body doesn’t feel like it got the message. I still feel drained before the day really starts. It makes work harder because carpentry already takes focus and you can’t be half-there with measurements and tools, but sometimes I’m just trying to get through the next task without sitting down and staring at nothing.\n\nSo yes, definitely decreased energy. It’s there even when I haven’t done enough to justify feeling that worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in being in the studio or working with wood, even though you used to find it fulfilling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s actually one of the harder parts to admit, because wood and the shop used to be the place where I felt most like myself.\n\nI used to walk in and even before starting anything, I’d notice the smell of the lumber, the grain, the little differences in a board, and there was some satisfaction in it. Even just setting up a cut or fitting pieces together used to feel… I don’t know, grounding. Like, this is something I know how to do, something I care about.\n\nLately, most days I wake up and it’s like this heavy gray feeling is already sitting on me before I even get moving. And then I get into the shop, and I’m surrounded by the same tools and the same wood I used to love working with, but it all feels flat. Like I’m looking at it from behind glass or something. I know, logically, that I used to enjoy it, and part of me still wants to feel that, but the feeling just doesn’t show up.\n\nSometimes I’ll stand there with a board in front of me or tools laid out, and I have to fight not to just stare at it. Not because I don’t know what to do, exactly, but because there’s no spark to start. It’s like the thing that used to pull me in now just sits there, and I’m forcing myself through the motions. So yes, I’ve noticed that loss of interest, or pleasure, or whatever you’d call it. It’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or waking up too early in the morning, even when you had the chance to get a full night's rest?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that one clearly. I mean, I go to bed, and I’m in bed for enough hours a lot of the time, but I haven’t really paid close attention to whether it’s taking me a long time to fall asleep or whether I’m waking up a lot. It’s not something I’ve tracked.\n\nThere are nights where I’m just lying there and I can’t tell if I’m actually awake for a long time or just sort of half-asleep, if that makes sense. And in the morning, I don’t always feel rested, but I’m not sure that means I didn’t sleep or that I woke up too early. I don’t usually look at the clock and think, “Oh, I’ve been awake since 3 a.m.” or anything that definite.\n\nSo… I guess I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear pattern of insomnia or waking up too early. Maybe my sleep isn’t great, but I can’t give you a confident yes on that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s more like the thought is there in the background when I’m really worn down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still, to the point that others might notice?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think anyone has said to me, “You’re moving slow,” or “You can’t sit still,” not in those words anyway.\n\nI mean, I’m not always a great judge of how I come across. Sometimes I feel like I’m just… dragging myself through things, but I don’t know if that means I’m actually moving slower in a way somebody else would notice, or if it’s more just how it feels inside. And with sitting still, I don’t think I’m pacing around all the time or anything like that. I might shift around or feel uncomfortable, but I don’t know that it’s obvious.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I haven’t really noticed it to that point, and I don’t think other people have pointed it out. If they have, I either didn’t take it that way or I wasn’t paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been keeping close enough track to say something definite, like “I lost this many pounds” or anything like that.\n\nMy appetite… I don’t know. Some days I eat kind of normally, or at least I think I do, and other days I realize I didn’t really pay attention to food much. But I can’t say for sure that it’s a big change, or that I’ve been eating much less than usual in some clear way. Nobody’s said I look thinner, and my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything.\n\nI guess if you’re asking whether I’ve noticed weight loss without trying, I don’t think so. Not that I’ve noticed. I probably wouldn’t be the best person to judge unless it was really obvious, but no, nothing specific comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your energy or feel fatigued most days, even when you haven’t done much physically or mentally?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That one I can answer pretty clearly. I feel tired most days, and it’s not the normal kind of tired where you can point to a hard day and say, well, that makes sense.\n\nIt’s like… by midmorning, sometimes my arms already feel like I’ve been hauling lumber all day, even if I haven’t done much yet. I’ll be trying to measure something or make a cut, or even just return a couple calls, and it feels like I’m pushing through mud. Like every little step has weight to it. And I’ll think, “How am I this wiped out already?” because it’s not even lunch.\n\nEven when I’ve been in bed a full night, it doesn’t really reset me. I can get up and technically I slept, but my body doesn’t feel like it got the message. I still feel drained before the day really starts. It makes work harder because carpentry already takes focus and you can’t be half-there with measurements and tools, but sometimes I’m just trying to get through the next task without sitting down and staring at nothing.\n\nSo yes, definitely decreased energy. It’s there even when I haven’t done enough to justify feeling that worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in being in the studio or working with wood, even though you used to find it fulfilling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s actually one of the harder parts to admit, because wood and the shop used to be the place where I felt most like myself.\n\nI used to walk in and even before starting anything, I’d notice the smell of the lumber, the grain, the little differences in a board, and there was some satisfaction in it. Even just setting up a cut or fitting pieces together used to feel… I don’t know, grounding. Like, this is something I know how to do, something I care about.\n\nLately, most days I wake up and it’s like this heavy gray feeling is already sitting on me before I even get moving. And then I get into the shop, and I’m surrounded by the same tools and the same wood I used to love working with, but it all feels flat. Like I’m looking at it from behind glass or something. I know, logically, that I used to enjoy it, and part of me still wants to feel that, but the feeling just doesn’t show up.\n\nSometimes I’ll stand there with a board in front of me or tools laid out, and I have to fight not to just stare at it. Not because I don’t know what to do, exactly, but because there’s no spark to start. It’s like the thing that used to pull me in now just sits there, and I’m forcing myself through the motions. So yes, I’ve noticed that loss of interest, or pleasure, or whatever you’d call it. It’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or waking up too early in the morning, even when you had the chance to get a full night's rest?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that one clearly. I mean, I go to bed, and I’m in bed for enough hours a lot of the time, but I haven’t really paid close attention to whether it’s taking me a long time to fall asleep or whether I’m waking up a lot. It’s not something I’ve tracked.\n\nThere are nights where I’m just lying there and I can’t tell if I’m actually awake for a long time or just sort of half-asleep, if that makes sense. And in the morning, I don’t always feel rested, but I’m not sure that means I didn’t sleep or that I woke up too early. I don’t usually look at the clock and think, “Oh, I’ve been awake since 3 a.m.” or anything that definite.\n\nSo… I guess I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear pattern of insomnia or waking up too early. Maybe my sleep isn’t great, but I can’t give you a confident yes on that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not really sure on that one. I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed some big, obvious drop in how much I’m eating, not in a way where I’d say, “Oh yeah, I barely eat anymore.” But I also don’t keep very close track of it, if I’m being honest.

Other days I might just grab whatever’s around and not think much about it, but that’s been true for a long time with work and everything. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly or watching my belt holes closely or anything like that, so I can’t say confidently whether I’ve lost weight without trying.

If I had dropped a lot, I think I’d probably notice my clothes fitting different or someone would say something, and that hasn’t really stood out to me. So… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed significant eating less or weight loss. Not clearly, anyway.

    "role": "system",

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I just… I don’t see a way forward anymore. I’ve been trying to keep things together, especially with the studio and the payments and all that, and every time somebody talks about another plan or another way to catch up, it just feels like words. Like it looks good on paper maybe, but in real life I can’t make it work.\n\nI’ve always been someone who figures things out by working harder, you know, putting in more hours, taking the job nobody else wants, fixing what’s broken piece by piece. But lately it feels like no matter how hard I work, nothing actually gets better. I can’t picture the studio getting back on track, and honestly I can’t really picture my life getting back on track either. It’s like I’m standing there looking at this mess and I know I’m supposed to say, “Okay, here’s the next step,” but I don’t believe there is one anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed if your mood has been consistently low or if you’ve lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. It’s not just a bad day here and there. Most mornings I wake up and it’s like this heavy, gray feeling is already there before I even open my eyes all the way. Like I haven’t even had a chance to think about the day yet, and my mood has already decided for me.\n\nAnd the stuff I used to care about… the shop, especially. I used to love being around the wood, the smell of it, looking at a rough piece and being able to picture what it could turn into. That used to settle me, honestly. But now I can be standing right there in the shop, surrounded by all of it, and it just feels flat. Like there’s no spark in it. I’ll pick something up and then just… stop. I have to fight not to just stand there staring, because nothing in me really connects to it the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like you’re not good enough or that things are your fault, even when others might not see it that way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s like, when something goes wrong at the studio, I don’t just see it as one thing that went wrong. If I’m fitting a joint and it doesn’t line up clean, or I measure something and it’s off, even a little, I don’t think, “Okay, fix the cut, adjust it, move on.” I go straight to, “What kind of carpenter can’t get this right?” And then it keeps going. Like, “Maybe I’m not as good as I thought I was. Maybe I’ve been fooling myself this whole time.”\n\nSame with the business side. If I miss a bid deadline or I don’t get back to someone fast enough, I don’t just think I messed up that one part. I start thinking I had no business trying to run my own studio in the first place. Like I took something I cared about and proved I wasn’t capable of handling it.\n\nOther people might say, you know, mistakes happen, or everybody gets behind. But in my head it feels bigger than that. It feels like it says something about me. Like I’m not dependable, or I’m not skilled enough, or I should’ve known better than to think I could make it work.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to live anymore or if it would just be easier if you weren’t around?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI mean, I hear what you’re asking, and I know it’s important, but when you say “not wanting to live anymore,” I’m not sure if what I feel counts as that, exactly. It’s more like… I get so tired of being in my own head, and tired of waking up to the same pile of things I don’t know how to fix. Sometimes the thought is less “I want to die” and more “I don’t want to have to keep doing this like this.” If that makes sense.\n\nAnd the “easier if I weren’t around” part… yeah, that thought kind of brushes up against me sometimes, I guess. Not in a way I’ve sat down and made sense of, and not something I know how to talk about without feeling ashamed. It’s just this ugly feeling that I’m taking up space or making things harder, and then I try to push it away because it scares me a little to even say it out loud.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I’m not sure what box that goes in. I just know I’ve been feeling worn down enough that those kinds of thoughts don’t feel as far away as they used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that more directly than I did. I don’t think I can say I’ve made some clear decision or anything like that. It’s more… the thoughts scare me because they get close to that line, and I don’t always know what to call them.\n\nHurting myself… I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there and thought it through in a concrete way, if that’s what you mean. But there are moments when I feel so worn down that I don’t feel like I trust my own thinking the way I used to. Like I’ll be standing there with all that heaviness in my chest, and it’s not exactly a plan, it’s not like I’m calmly deciding something, but there’s this thought of, “I can’t keep feeling like this.” And then I get ashamed for even having that thought.\n\nEnding my life… saying it that way makes my stomach drop. I don’t want to tell you yes if yes means I’m planning something, because I’m not saying that. But I also don’t want to lie and make it sound like everything in my head is safe and tidy, because it isn’t. It’s more like those thoughts come near me sometimes, and I try to back away from them. I don’t really know how dangerous that makes it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that the only way to escape how you’re feeling is to end your life, even if you haven’t made a specific plan?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say it exactly like that. The “only way” part feels… I don’t know, it feels too definite, like I’ve sat there and come to a conclusion, and I don’t think I have. I haven’t made a plan, and I’m not sitting here thinking, “This is what I’m going to do.”\n\nBut I have had moments where it feels like I just want out. Out of the feeling, out of the pressure in my head, out of waking up and already feeling like I’m behind or failing before I’ve even done anything. And in those moments, my mind kind of goes toward that dark edge, where it feels like maybe there isn’t another way to get relief. I don’t like saying that. It makes me feel ashamed, and it scares me.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it believing ending my life is the only way, exactly. But I’ve been close enough to that kind of thought that I can’t honestly say it never happens. It’s more like the thought shows up as an escape hatch, and then I try not to look at it too long.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever started thinking about how you might end your life, even if you didn’t go through with it or make a firm plan?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I don’t think I have anything clear to tell you there.\n\nLike, I haven’t sat down and thought, “This is how I would do it,” or worked anything out in my head. At least not in a way that feels like a plan. Sometimes my mind gets kind of close to that area and then I almost pull back from it, because it feels like if I keep going down that road, it’s going to become more real than I want it to be.\n\nIt’s hard because when you ask “how,” part of me wants to say no, because I don’t have some specific answer. But another part of me worries that even being near those thoughts means something. I don’t know if that makes sense. It’s more like I get overwhelmed and want everything to stop, and then I try not to let my mind fill in the blanks.\n\nSo… no firm plan. I don’t think I’ve really let myself think it through like that. But I’m scared that the thoughts have gotten close enough that I’m even having to answer this question.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself thinking about death or dying, even if you didn’t have a plan or intend to do anything about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to separate that from the other stuff, honestly. Death… dying… those words float through my head sometimes, but I don’t always know if it’s me thinking about death or just thinking about not having to keep doing this. If that makes sense.\n\nI don’t sit there imagining some clear scene or anything like that. It’s more vague. Like I’ll be driving home, or lying there when everything’s quiet, and the thought is just… “What if I didn’t have to wake up tomorrow?” And then I feel this horrible mix of relief and fear, and then guilt for even letting that thought exist. It’s not like I’m saying I want to act on it. I don’t think that’s what I mean. But yeah, I guess death comes into my mind as this kind of blank ending, like a way for the noise to stop.\n\nI hate admitting that because it sounds worse out loud than it feels in my head sometimes. In my head it can be almost passive, like I’m not reaching for it, it just shows up. But then when you ask me directly, I can hear how serious it is. So… yes, I’ve thought about death or dying, but not in a way where I’ve made a plan or decided I’m going to do something. It’s more like the thought is there in the background when I’m really worn down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still, to the point that others might notice?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think anyone has said to me, “You’re moving slow,” or “You can’t sit still,” not in those words anyway.\n\nI mean, I’m not always a great judge of how I come across. Sometimes I feel like I’m just… dragging myself through things, but I don’t know if that means I’m actually moving slower in a way somebody else would notice, or if it’s more just how it feels inside. And with sitting still, I don’t think I’m pacing around all the time or anything like that. I might shift around or feel uncomfortable, but I don’t know that it’s obvious.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I haven’t really noticed it to that point, and I don’t think other people have pointed it out. If they have, I either didn’t take it that way or I wasn’t paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been keeping close enough track to say something definite, like “I lost this many pounds” or anything like that.\n\nMy appetite… I don’t know. Some days I eat kind of normally, or at least I think I do, and other days I realize I didn’t really pay attention to food much. But I can’t say for sure that it’s a big change, or that I’ve been eating much less than usual in some clear way. Nobody’s said I look thinner, and my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything.\n\nI guess if you’re asking whether I’ve noticed weight loss without trying, I don’t think so. Not that I’ve noticed. I probably wouldn’t be the best person to judge unless it was really obvious, but no, nothing specific comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your energy or feel fatigued most days, even when you haven’t done much physically or mentally?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That one I can answer pretty clearly. I feel tired most days, and it’s not the normal kind of tired where you can point to a hard day and say, well, that makes sense.\n\nIt’s like… by midmorning, sometimes my arms already feel like I’ve been hauling lumber all day, even if I haven’t done much yet. I’ll be trying to measure something or make a cut, or even just return a couple calls, and it feels like I’m pushing through mud. Like every little step has weight to it. And I’ll think, “How am I this wiped out already?” because it’s not even lunch.\n\nEven when I’ve been in bed a full night, it doesn’t really reset me. I can get up and technically I slept, but my body doesn’t feel like it got the message. I still feel drained before the day really starts. It makes work harder because carpentry already takes focus and you can’t be half-there with measurements and tools, but sometimes I’m just trying to get through the next task without sitting down and staring at nothing.\n\nSo yes, definitely decreased energy. It’s there even when I haven’t done enough to justify feeling that worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in being in the studio or working with wood, even though you used to find it fulfilling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s actually one of the harder parts to admit, because wood and the shop used to be the place where I felt most like myself.\n\nI used to walk in and even before starting anything, I’d notice the smell of the lumber, the grain, the little differences in a board, and there was some satisfaction in it. Even just setting up a cut or fitting pieces together used to feel… I don’t know, grounding. Like, this is something I know how to do, something I care about.\n\nLately, most days I wake up and it’s like this heavy gray feeling is already sitting on me before I even get moving. And then I get into the shop, and I’m surrounded by the same tools and the same wood I used to love working with, but it all feels flat. Like I’m looking at it from behind glass or something. I know, logically, that I used to enjoy it, and part of me still wants to feel that, but the feeling just doesn’t show up.\n\nSometimes I’ll stand there with a board in front of me or tools laid out, and I have to fight not to just stare at it. Not because I don’t know what to do, exactly, but because there’s no spark to start. It’s like the thing that used to pull me in now just sits there, and I’m forcing myself through the motions. So yes, I’ve noticed that loss of interest, or pleasure, or whatever you’d call it. It’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or waking up too early in the morning, even when you had the chance to get a full night's rest?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that one clearly. I mean, I go to bed, and I’m in bed for enough hours a lot of the time, but I haven’t really paid close attention to whether it’s taking me a long time to fall asleep or whether I’m waking up a lot. It’s not something I’ve tracked.\n\nThere are nights where I’m just lying there and I can’t tell if I’m actually awake for a long time or just sort of half-asleep, if that makes sense. And in the morning, I don’t always feel rested, but I’m not sure that means I didn’t sleep or that I woke up too early. I don’t usually look at the clock and think, “Oh, I’ve been awake since 3 a.m.” or anything that definite.\n\nSo… I guess I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear pattern of insomnia or waking up too early. Maybe my sleep isn’t great, but I can’t give you a confident yes on that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself eating significantly less than usual, or have you lost any weight without actively trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure on that one. I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed some big, obvious drop in how much I’m eating, not in a way where I’d say, “Oh yeah, I barely eat anymore.” But I also don’t keep very close track of it, if I’m being honest.\n\nSome days I eat kind of normally, or at least what feels normal for me. Other days I might just grab whatever’s around and not think much about it, but that’s been true for a long time with work and everything. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly or watching my belt holes closely or anything like that, so I can’t say confidently whether I’ve lost weight without trying.\n\nIf I had dropped a lot, I think I’d probably notice my clothes fitting different or someone would say something, and that hasn’t really stood out to me. So… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed significant eating less or weight loss. Not clearly, anyway.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And then I get into the shop, and I’m surrounded by the same tools and the same wood I used to love working with, but it all feels flat. Like I’m looking at it from behind glass or something. I know, logically, that I used to enjoy it, and part of me still wants to feel that, but the feeling just doesn’t show up.\n\nSometimes I’ll stand there with a board in front of me or tools laid out, and I have to fight not to just stare at it. Not because I don’t know what to do, exactly, but because there’s no spark to start. It’s like the thing that used to pull me in now just sits there, and I’m forcing myself through the motions. So yes, I’ve noticed that loss of interest, or pleasure, or whatever you’d call it. It’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or waking up too early in the morning, even when you had the chance to get a full night's rest?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that one clearly. I mean, I go to bed, and I’m in bed for enough hours a lot of the time, but I haven’t really paid close attention to whether it’s taking me a long time to fall asleep or whether I’m waking up a lot. It’s not something I’ve tracked.\n\nThere are nights where I’m just lying there and I can’t tell if I’m actually awake for a long time or just sort of half-asleep, if that makes sense. And in the morning, I don’t always feel rested, but I’m not sure that means I didn’t sleep or that I woke up too early. I don’t usually look at the clock and think, “Oh, I’ve been awake since 3 a.m.” or anything that definite.\n\nSo… I guess I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear pattern of insomnia or waking up too early. Maybe my sleep isn’t great, but I can’t give you a confident yes on that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself eating significantly less than usual, or have you lost any weight without actively trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure on that one. I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed some big, obvious drop in how much I’m eating, not in a way where I’d say, “Oh yeah, I barely eat anymore.” But I also don’t keep very close track of it, if I’m being honest.\n\nSome days I eat kind of normally, or at least what feels normal for me. Other days I might just grab whatever’s around and not think much about it, but that’s been true for a long time with work and everything. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly or watching my belt holes closely or anything like that, so I can’t say confidently whether I’ve lost weight without trying.\n\nIf I had dropped a lot, I think I’d probably notice my clothes fitting different or someone would say something, and that hasn’t really stood out to me. So… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed significant eating less or weight loss.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF32.2, F32.1, F32.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I just… I don’t see a way forward anymore. I’ve been trying to keep things together, especially with the studio and the payments and all that, and every time somebody talks about another plan or another way to catch up, it just feels like words. Like it looks good on paper maybe, but in real life I can’t make it work.\n\nI’ve always been someone who figures things out by working harder, you know, putting in more hours, taking the job nobody else wants, fixing what’s broken piece by piece. But lately it feels like no matter how hard I work, nothing actually gets better. I can’t picture the studio getting back on track, and honestly I can’t really picture my life getting back on track either. It’s like I’m standing there looking at this mess and I know I’m supposed to say, “Okay, here’s the next step,” but I don’t believe there is one anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed if your mood has been consistently low or if you’ve lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. It’s not just a bad day here and there. Most mornings I wake up and it’s like this heavy, gray feeling is already there before I even open my eyes all the way. Like I haven’t even had a chance to think about the day yet, and my mood has already decided for me.\n\nAnd the stuff I used to care about… the shop, especially. I used to love being around the wood, the smell of it, looking at a rough piece and being able to picture what it could turn into. That used to settle me, honestly. But now I can be standing right there in the shop, surrounded by all of it, and it just feels flat. Like there’s no spark in it. I’ll pick something up and then just… stop. I have to fight not to just stand there staring, because nothing in me really connects to it the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling like you’re not good enough or that things are your fault, even when others might not see it that way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Probably more than I should, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s like, when something goes wrong at the studio, I don’t just see it as one thing that went wrong. If I’m fitting a joint and it doesn’t line up clean, or I measure something and it’s off, even a little, I don’t think, “Okay, fix the cut, adjust it, move on.” I go straight to, “What kind of carpenter can’t get this right?” And then it keeps going. Like, “Maybe I’m not as good as I thought I was. Maybe I’ve been fooling myself this whole time.”\n\nSame with the business side. If I miss a bid deadline or I don’t get back to someone fast enough, I don’t just think I messed up that one part. I start thinking I had no business trying to run my own studio in the first place. Like I took something I cared about and proved I wasn’t capable of handling it.\n\nOther people might say, you know, mistakes happen, or everybody gets behind. But in my head it feels bigger than that. It feels like it says something about me. Like I’m not dependable, or I’m not skilled enough, or I should’ve known better than to think I could make it work.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about not wanting to live anymore or if it would just be easier if you weren’t around?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI mean, I hear what you’re asking, and I know it’s important, but when you say “not wanting to live anymore,” I’m not sure if what I feel counts as that, exactly. It’s more like… I get so tired of being in my own head, and tired of waking up to the same pile of things I don’t know how to fix. Sometimes the thought is less “I want to die” and more “I don’t want to have to keep doing this like this.” If that makes sense.\n\nAnd the “easier if I weren’t around” part… yeah, that thought kind of brushes up against me sometimes, I guess. Not in a way I’ve sat down and made sense of, and not something I know how to talk about without feeling ashamed. It’s just this ugly feeling that I’m taking up space or making things harder, and then I try to push it away because it scares me a little to even say it out loud.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I’m not sure what box that goes in. I just know I’ve been feeling worn down enough that those kinds of thoughts don’t feel as far away as they used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that more directly than I did. I don’t think I can say I’ve made some clear decision or anything like that. It’s more… the thoughts scare me because they get close to that line, and I don’t always know what to call them.\n\nHurting myself… I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there and thought it through in a concrete way, if that’s what you mean. But there are moments when I feel so worn down that I don’t feel like I trust my own thinking the way I used to. Like I’ll be standing there with all that heaviness in my chest, and it’s not exactly a plan, it’s not like I’m calmly deciding something, but there’s this thought of, “I can’t keep feeling like this.” And then I get ashamed for even having that thought.\n\nEnding my life… saying it that way makes my stomach drop. I don’t want to tell you yes if yes means I’m planning something, because I’m not saying that. But I also don’t want to lie and make it sound like everything in my head is safe and tidy, because it isn’t. It’s more like those thoughts come near me sometimes, and I try to back away from them. I don’t really know how dangerous that makes it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that the only way to escape how you’re feeling is to end your life, even if you haven’t made a specific plan?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say it exactly like that. The “only way” part feels… I don’t know, it feels too definite, like I’ve sat there and come to a conclusion, and I don’t think I have. I haven’t made a plan, and I’m not sitting here thinking, “This is what I’m going to do.”\n\nBut I have had moments where it feels like I just want out. Out of the feeling, out of the pressure in my head, out of waking up and already feeling like I’m behind or failing before I’ve even done anything. And in those moments, my mind kind of goes toward that dark edge, where it feels like maybe there isn’t another way to get relief. I don’t like saying that. It makes me feel ashamed, and it scares me.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it believing ending my life is the only way, exactly. But I’ve been close enough to that kind of thought that I can’t honestly say it never happens. It’s more like the thought shows up as an escape hatch, and then I try not to look at it too long.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever started thinking about how you might end your life, even if you didn’t go through with it or make a firm plan?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I don’t think I have anything clear to tell you there.\n\nLike, I haven’t sat down and thought, “This is how I would do it,” or worked anything out in my head. At least not in a way that feels like a plan. Sometimes my mind gets kind of close to that area and then I almost pull back from it, because it feels like if I keep going down that road, it’s going to become more real than I want it to be.\n\nIt’s hard because when you ask “how,” part of me wants to say no, because I don’t have some specific answer. But another part of me worries that even being near those thoughts means something. I don’t know if that makes sense. It’s more like I get overwhelmed and want everything to stop, and then I try not to let my mind fill in the blanks.\n\nSo… no firm plan. I don’t think I’ve really let myself think it through like that. But I’m scared that the thoughts have gotten close enough that I’m even having to answer this question.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself thinking about death or dying, even if you didn’t have a plan or intend to do anything about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to separate that from the other stuff, honestly. Death… dying… those words float through my head sometimes, but I don’t always know if it’s me thinking about death or just thinking about not having to keep doing this. If that makes sense.\n\nI don’t sit there imagining some clear scene or anything like that. It’s more vague. Like I’ll be driving home, or lying there when everything’s quiet, and the thought is just… “What if I didn’t have to wake up tomorrow?” And then I feel this horrible mix of relief and fear, and then guilt for even letting that thought exist. It’s not like I’m saying I want to act on it. I don’t think that’s what I mean. But yeah, I guess death comes into my mind as this kind of blank ending, like a way for the noise to stop.\n\nI hate admitting that because it sounds worse out loud than it feels in my head sometimes. In my head it can be almost passive, like I’m not reaching for it, it just shows up. But then when you ask me directly, I can hear how serious it is. So… yes, I’ve thought about death or dying, but not in a way where I’ve made a plan or decided I’m going to do something. It’s more like the thought is there in the background when I’m really worn down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still, to the point that others might notice?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think anyone has said to me, “You’re moving slow,” or “You can’t sit still,” not in those words anyway.\n\nI mean, I’m not always a great judge of how I come across. Sometimes I feel like I’m just… dragging myself through things, but I don’t know if that means I’m actually moving slower in a way somebody else would notice, or if it’s more just how it feels inside. And with sitting still, I don’t think I’m pacing around all the time or anything like that. I might shift around or feel uncomfortable, but I don’t know that it’s obvious.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I haven’t really noticed it to that point, and I don’t think other people have pointed it out. Some days I eat kind of normally, or at least I think I do, and other days I realize I didn’t really pay attention to food much. Not that I’ve noticed. I probably wouldn’t be the best person to judge unless it was really obvious, but no, nothing specific comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your energy or feel fatigued most days, even when you haven’t done much physically or mentally?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That one I can answer pretty clearly. I feel tired most days, and it’s not the normal kind of tired where you can point to a hard day and say, well, that makes sense.\n\nIt’s like… by midmorning, sometimes my arms already feel like I’ve been hauling lumber all day, even if I haven’t done much yet. I’ll be trying to measure something or make a cut, or even just return a couple calls, and it feels like I’m pushing through mud. Like every little step has weight to it. And I’ll think, “How am I this wiped out already?” because it’s not even lunch.\n\nEven when I’ve been in bed a full night, it doesn’t really reset me. I can get up and technically I slept, but my body doesn’t feel like it got the message. I still feel drained before the day really starts. It makes work harder because carpentry already takes focus and you can’t be half-there with measurements and tools, but sometimes I’m just trying to get through the next task without sitting down and staring at nothing.\n\nSo yes, definitely decreased energy. It’s there even when I haven’t done enough to justify feeling that worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in being in the studio or working with wood, even though you used to find it fulfilling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s actually one of the harder parts to admit, because wood and the shop used to be the place where I felt most like myself.\n\nI used to walk in and even before starting anything, I’d notice the smell of the lumber, the grain, the little differences in a board, and there was some satisfaction in it. Even just setting up a cut or fitting pieces together used to feel… I don’t know, grounding. Like, this is something I know how to do, something I care about.\n\nLately, most days I wake up and it’s like this heavy gray feeling is already sitting on me before I even get moving. And then I get into the shop, and I’m surrounded by the same tools and the same wood I used to love working with, but it all feels flat. Like I’m looking at it from behind glass or something. I know, logically, that I used to enjoy it, and part of me still wants to feel that, but the feeling just doesn’t show up.\n\nSometimes I’ll stand there with a board in front of me or tools laid out, and I have to fight not to just stare at it. Not because I don’t know what to do, exactly, but because there’s no spark to start. It’s like the thing that used to pull me in now just sits there, and I’m forcing myself through the motions. So yes, I’ve noticed that loss of interest, or pleasure, or whatever you’d call it. It’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or waking up too early in the morning, even when you had the chance to get a full night's rest?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that one clearly. I mean, I go to bed, and I’m in bed for enough hours a lot of the time, but I haven’t really paid close attention to whether it’s taking me a long time to fall asleep or whether I’m waking up a lot. It’s not something I’ve tracked.\n\nThere are nights where I’m just lying there and I can’t tell if I’m actually awake for a long time or just sort of half-asleep, if that makes sense. And in the morning, I don’t always feel rested, but I’m not sure that means I didn’t sleep or that I woke up too early. I don’t usually look at the clock and think, “Oh, I’ve been awake since 3 a.m.” or anything that definite.\n\nSo… I guess I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear pattern of insomnia or waking up too early. Maybe my sleep isn’t great, but I can’t give you a confident yes on that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself eating significantly less than usual, or have you lost any weight without actively trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure on that one. I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed some big, obvious drop in how much I’m eating, not in a way where I’d say, “Oh yeah, I barely eat anymore.” But I also don’t keep very close track of it, if I’m being honest.\n\nSome days I eat kind of normally, or at least what feels normal for me. Other days I might just grab whatever’s around and not think much about it, but that’s been true for a long time with work and everything. But when you say “the only way to escape is to end your life,” I don’t think that’s where my mind has gone.\n\nIt’s hard to answer because the feeling can get pretty heavy, and I don’t want to make it sound lighter than it is. But I also don’t want to say yes to something that isn’t really what I’ve experienced. I haven’t been sitting there thinking through ending my life as an escape. It’s more like… I want relief. I want the weight of it to let up. But no, not in the sense of wanting to end my life.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I think about things being exhausting or pointless sometimes, but when you say death or dying outside of being really overwhelmed… I don’t know. That feels like a pretty specific thing, and I don’t think I’ve noticed my mind going there in that way.

I don’t sit around thinking about dying, at least not that I’m aware of. If something comes up on the news, or someone mentions someone passing away, sure, I might think about it like anybody would, but I don’t think that’s what you mean. I haven’t had these separate thoughts about death just showing up on their own, like when I’m calm or just going about my day.

So I guess my answer would be no, or at least not clearly. I’m trying not to overstate it. It’s more that I get worn down by how things feel, but I don’t think I’ve been thinking about death or dying in that way.

I’ve been trying to keep things together, especially with the studio and the payments and all that, and every time somebody talks about another plan or another way to catch up, it just feels like words. It’s like I’m standing there looking at this mess and I know I’m supposed to say, “Okay, here’s the next step,” but I don’t believe there is one anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed if your mood has been consistently low or if you’ve lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have. It’s not just a bad day here and there. Most mornings I wake up and it’s like this heavy, gray feeling is already there before I even open my eyes all the way. Like I haven’t even had a chance to think about the day yet, and my mood has already decided for me.\n\nAnd the stuff I used to care about… the shop, especially. I used to love being around the wood, the smell of it, looking at a rough piece and being able to picture what it could turn into. That used to settle me, honestly. But now I can be standing right there in the shop, surrounded by all of it, and it just feels flat. Like there’s no spark in it. I’ll pick something up and then just… stop. Probably more than I should, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s like, when something goes wrong at the studio, I don’t just see it as one thing that went wrong. If I’m fitting a joint and it doesn’t line up clean, or I measure something and it’s off, even a little, I don’t think, “Okay, fix the cut, adjust it, move on.” I go straight to, “What kind of carpenter can’t get this right?” And then it keeps going. Not in a way I’ve sat down and made sense of, and not something I know how to talk about without feeling ashamed. Like I’ll be standing there with all that heaviness in my chest, and it’s not exactly a plan, it’s not like I’m calmly deciding something, but there’s this thought of, “I can’t keep feeling like this.” And then I get ashamed for even having that thought.\n\nEnding my life… saying it that way makes my stomach drop. I don’t want to tell you yes if yes means I’m planning something, because I’m not saying that. But I also don’t want to lie and make it sound like everything in my head is safe and tidy, because it isn’t. It’s more like those thoughts come near me sometimes, and I try to back away from them. I don’t really know how dangerous that makes it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that the only way to escape how you’re feeling is to end your life, even if you haven’t made a specific plan?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say it exactly like that. The “only way” part feels… I don’t know, it feels too definite, like I’ve sat there and come to a conclusion, and I don’t think I have. I haven’t made a plan, and I’m not sitting here thinking, “This is what I’m going to do.”\n\nBut I have had moments where it feels like I just want out. Out of the feeling, out of the pressure in my head, out of waking up and already feeling like I’m behind or failing before I’ve even done anything. And in those moments, my mind kind of goes toward that dark edge, where it feels like maybe there isn’t another way to get relief. I don’t like saying that. It makes me feel ashamed, and it scares me.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it believing ending my life is the only way, exactly. But I’ve been close enough to that kind of thought that I can’t honestly say it never happens. It’s more like the thought shows up as an escape hatch, and then I try not to look at it too long.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever started thinking about how you might end your life, even if you didn’t go through with it or make a firm plan?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. Sometimes my mind gets kind of close to that area and then I almost pull back from it, because it feels like if I keep going down that road, it’s going to become more real than I want it to be.\n\nIt’s hard because when you ask “how,” part of me wants to say no, because I don’t have some specific answer. But another part of me worries that even being near those thoughts means something. I don’t know if that makes sense. Not that I’ve noticed. I probably wouldn’t be the best person to judge unless it was really obvious, but no, nothing specific comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your energy or feel fatigued most days, even when you haven’t done much physically or mentally?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That one I can answer pretty clearly. I feel tired most days, and it’s not the normal kind of tired where you can point to a hard day and say, well, that makes sense.\n\nIt’s like… by midmorning, sometimes my arms already feel like I’ve been hauling lumber all day, even if I haven’t done much yet. I’ll be trying to measure something or make a cut, or even just return a couple calls, and it feels like I’m pushing through mud. Like every little step has weight to it. And I’ll think, “How am I this wiped out already?” because it’s not even lunch.\n\nEven when I’ve been in bed a full night, it doesn’t really reset me. I can get up and technically I slept, but my body doesn’t feel like it got the message. I still feel drained before the day really starts. It makes work harder because carpentry already takes focus and you can’t be half-there with measurements and tools, but sometimes I’m just trying to get through the next task without sitting down and staring at nothing.\n\nSo yes, definitely decreased energy. It’s there even when I haven’t done enough to justify feeling that worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in being in the studio or working with wood, even though you used to find it fulfilling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s actually one of the harder parts to admit, because wood and the shop used to be the place where I felt most like myself.\n\nI used to walk in and even before starting anything, I’d notice the smell of the lumber, the grain, the little differences in a board, and there was some satisfaction in it. Even just setting up a cut or fitting pieces together used to feel… I don’t know, grounding. Like, this is something I know how to do, something I care about.\n\nLately, most days I wake up and it’s like this heavy gray feeling is already sitting on me before I even get moving. And then I get into the shop, and I’m surrounded by the same tools and the same wood I used to love working with, but it all feels flat. Like I’m looking at it from behind glass or something. I know, logically, that I used to enjoy it, and part of me still wants to feel that, but the feeling just doesn’t show up.\n\nSometimes I’ll stand there with a board in front of me or tools laid out, and I have to fight not to just stare at it. Not because I don’t know what to do, exactly, but because there’s no spark to start. It’s like the thing that used to pull me in now just sits there, and I’m forcing myself through the motions. So yes, I’ve noticed that loss of interest, or pleasure, or whatever you’d call it. It’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or waking up too early in the morning, even when you had the chance to get a full night's rest?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that one clearly. I mean, I go to bed, and I’m in bed for enough hours a lot of the time, but I haven’t really paid close attention to whether it’s taking me a long time to fall asleep or whether I’m waking up a lot. It’s not something I’ve tracked.\n\nThere are nights where I’m just lying there and I can’t tell if I’m actually awake for a long time or just sort of half-asleep, if that makes sense. I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed some big, obvious drop in how much I’m eating, not in a way where I’d say, “Oh yeah, I barely eat anymore.” But I also don’t keep very close track of it, if I’m being honest.\n\nSome days I eat kind of normally, or at least what feels normal for me. Other days I might just grab whatever’s around and not think much about it, but that’s been true for a long time with work and everything. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly or watching my belt holes closely or anything like that, so I can’t say confidently whether I’ve lost weight without trying.\n\nIf I had dropped a lot, I think I’d probably notice my clothes fitting different or someone would say something, and that hasn’t really stood out to me. So… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed significant eating less or weight loss. I don’t think I have anything clear to tell you there.\n\nLike, I haven’t sat down and thought, “This is how I would do it,” or worked anything out in my head. At least not in a way that feels like a plan. Sometimes my mind gets kind of close to that area and then I almost pull back from it, because it feels like if I keep going down that road, it’s going to become more real than I want it to be.\n\nIt’s hard because when you ask “how,” part of me wants to say no, because I don’t have some specific answer. But another part of me worries that even being near those thoughts means something. I don’t know if that makes sense. It’s more like I get overwhelmed and want everything to stop, and then I try not to let my mind fill in the blanks.\n\nSo… no firm plan. I don’t think I’ve really let myself think it through like that. But I’m scared that the thoughts have gotten close enough that I’m even having to answer this question.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself thinking about death or dying, even if you didn’t have a plan or intend to do anything about it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to separate that from the other stuff, honestly. Death… dying… those words float through my head sometimes, but I don’t always know if it’s me thinking about death or just thinking about not having to keep doing this. If that makes sense.\n\nI don’t sit there imagining some clear scene or anything like that. It’s more vague. Like I’ll be driving home, or lying there when everything’s quiet, and the thought is just… “What if I didn’t have to wake up tomorrow?” And then I feel this horrible mix of relief and fear, and then guilt for even letting that thought exist. It’s not like I’m saying I want to act on it. I don’t think that’s what I mean. But yeah, I guess death comes into my mind as this kind of blank ending, like a way for the noise to stop.\n\nI hate admitting that because it sounds worse out loud than it feels in my head sometimes. In my head it can be almost passive, like I’m not reaching for it, it just shows up. But then when you ask me directly, I can hear how serious it is. So… yes, I’ve thought about death or dying, but not in a way where I’ve made a plan or decided I’m going to do something. It’s more like the thought is there in the background when I’m really worn down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or have trouble sitting still, to the point that others might notice?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think anyone has said to me, “You’re moving slow,” or “You can’t sit still,” not in those words anyway.\n\nI mean, I’m not always a great judge of how I come across. Sometimes I feel like I’m just… dragging myself through things, but I don’t know if that means I’m actually moving slower in a way somebody else would notice, or if it’s more just how it feels inside. And with sitting still, I don’t think I’m pacing around all the time or anything like that. I might shift around or feel uncomfortable, but I don’t know that it’s obvious.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I haven’t really noticed it to that point, and I don’t think other people have pointed it out. If they have, I either didn’t take it that way or I wasn’t paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently, like eating much less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been keeping close enough track to say something definite, like “I lost this many pounds” or anything like that.\n\nMy appetite… I don’t know. Some days I eat kind of normally, or at least I think I do, and other days I realize I didn’t really pay attention to food much. But I can’t say for sure that it’s a big change, or that I’ve been eating much less than usual in some clear way. Nobody’s said I look thinner, and my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything.\n\nI guess if you’re asking whether I’ve noticed weight loss without trying, I don’t think so. Not that I’ve noticed. I probably wouldn’t be the best person to judge unless it was really obvious, but no, nothing specific comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in your energy or feel fatigued most days, even when you haven’t done much physically or mentally?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That one I can answer pretty clearly. I feel tired most days, and it’s not the normal kind of tired where you can point to a hard day and say, well, that makes sense.\n\nIt’s like… by midmorning, sometimes my arms already feel like I’ve been hauling lumber all day, even if I haven’t done much yet. I’ll be trying to measure something or make a cut, or even just return a couple calls, and it feels like I’m pushing through mud. Like every little step has weight to it. And I’ll think, “How am I this wiped out already?” because it’s not even lunch.\n\nEven when I’ve been in bed a full night, it doesn’t really reset me. I can get up and technically I slept, but my body doesn’t feel like it got the message. I still feel drained before the day really starts. It makes work harder because carpentry already takes focus and you can’t be half-there with measurements and tools, but sometimes I’m just trying to get through the next task without sitting down and staring at nothing.\n\nSo yes, definitely decreased energy. It’s there even when I haven’t done enough to justify feeling that worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in being in the studio or working with wood, even though you used to find it fulfilling?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s actually one of the harder parts to admit, because wood and the shop used to be the place where I felt most like myself.\n\nI used to walk in and even before starting anything, I’d notice the smell of the lumber, the grain, the little differences in a board, and there was some satisfaction in it. Even just setting up a cut or fitting pieces together used to feel… I don’t know, grounding. Like, this is something I know how to do, something I care about.\n\nLately, most days I wake up and it’s like this heavy gray feeling is already sitting on me before I even get moving. And then I get into the shop, and I’m surrounded by the same tools and the same wood I used to love working with, but it all feels flat. Like I’m looking at it from behind glass or something. I know, logically, that I used to enjoy it, and part of me still wants to feel that, but the feeling just doesn’t show up.\n\nSometimes I’ll stand there with a board in front of me or tools laid out, and I have to fight not to just stare at it. Not because I don’t know what to do, exactly, but because there’s no spark to start. It’s like the thing that used to pull me in now just sits there, and I’m forcing myself through the motions. So yes, I’ve noticed that loss of interest, or pleasure, or whatever you’d call it. It’s there most days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or waking up too early in the morning, even when you had the chance to get a full night's rest?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that one clearly. I mean, I go to bed, and I’m in bed for enough hours a lot of the time, but I haven’t really paid close attention to whether it’s taking me a long time to fall asleep or whether I’m waking up a lot. It’s not something I’ve tracked.\n\nThere are nights where I’m just lying there and I can’t tell if I’m actually awake for a long time or just sort of half-asleep, if that makes sense. And in the morning, I don’t always feel rested, but I’m not sure that means I didn’t sleep or that I woke up too early. I don’t usually look at the clock and think, “Oh, I’ve been awake since 3 a.m.” or anything that definite.\n\nSo… I guess I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a clear pattern of insomnia or waking up too early. Maybe my sleep isn’t great, but I can’t give you a confident yes on that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself eating significantly less than usual, or have you lost any weight without actively trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure on that one. I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed some big, obvious drop in how much I’m eating, not in a way where I’d say, “Oh yeah, I barely eat anymore.” But I also don’t keep very close track of it, if I’m being honest.\n\nSome days I eat kind of normally, or at least what feels normal for me. Other days I might just grab whatever’s around and not think much about it, but that’s been true for a long time with work and everything. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly or watching my belt holes closely or anything like that, so I can’t say confidently whether I’ve lost weight without trying.\n\nIf I had dropped a lot, I think I’d probably notice my clothes fitting different or someone would say something, and that hasn’t really stood out to me. So… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed significant eating less or weight loss. Not clearly, anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that the only way to escape how you’re feeling is to end your life, even if you haven’t made a specific plan?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think I can say that I’ve felt that exactly. But when you say “the only way to escape is to end your life,” I don’t think that’s where my mind has gone.\n\nIt’s hard to answer because the feeling can get pretty heavy, and I don’t want to make it sound lighter than it is. But I also don’t want to say yes to something that isn’t really what I’ve experienced. I haven’t been sitting there thinking through ending my life as an escape. It’s more like… I want relief. I want the weight of it to let up. But no, not in the sense of wanting to end my life.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself thinking about death or dying outside of moments when you feel overwhelmed, even if you don’t intend to act on those thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. If something comes up on the news, or someone mentions someone passing away, sure, I might think about it like anybody would, but I don’t think that’s what you mean. I haven’t had these separate thoughts about death just showing up on their own, like when I’m calm or just going about my day.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be no, or at least not clearly. I’m not really sure, honestly. I don’t think anyone’s said to me, like, “You’re moving slow,” or “Why are you so restless?” Nothing that direct.

I mean, I have days where I feel kind of slowed down inside, if that makes sense, but whether it actually shows on the outside to where other people would notice… I don’t know. With carpentry, I’m usually just trying to get through what needs doing, and if I’m tired or dragging, I might just assume that’s normal for the day. I haven’t had someone point out that I’m walking slower or talking slower or anything like that.

And trouble sitting still… not really in a way I’d identify as a problem. I might shift around or get up if I’ve been sitting too long, but I wouldn’t say it’s this obvious thing other people would notice.

So I guess I’d say no, not that I’ve noticed. Or at least I can’t confidently say that’s been happening.
